--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- Champion: `-1.71` — single state GoToColor with `--clr0 2` (GREEN walls). This means robots go toward GREEN walls, pick up items from the GREEN half, and deliver them to the nest. Since the score is -1.71, these items must be the "good" ones (decrease score by 1).
- The key insight: `--clr0 2` = GREEN. GoToColor toward GREEN walls works well. The single-state machine just keeps going to green indefinitely.
- `--clr0 1` would be RED (nest wall) — that could cause issues or help return.
- The worst: `--clr0 3` = BLUE, which gave score +10.32. So BLUE items INCREASE the score — we must avoid blue!

**Strategy:**
The champion (GoToColor GREEN) scores -1.71. We want to improve this. Key ideas:
1. Robots need to: go to green source → pick up item → return to nest (white floor) → deliver.
2. The champion works because GoToColor(GREEN) keeps robots near the green half, items get picked up, and eventually robots wander to nest.
3. Better: add a state that goes toward RED wall (nest side) when carrying an item, triggered by being on black floor (at source).

**Color mapping:**
- clr=1: RED (nest wall)
- clr=2: GREEN (good source)  
- clr=3: BLUE (bad source — AVOID)
- clr=4: YELLOW (robot LEDs?)
- clr=5: MAGENTA
- clr=6: CYAN

**Floor colors:**
- BLACK = source area
- WHITE = nest area
- GRAY = corridor

**Two-state machines:**
- State 0: GoToColor(GREEN, clr=2) → when on black floor (at source), transition to state 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED, clr=1) → go to nest wall → when on white floor, back to state 0

**Also try:** GoAwayColor(BLUE) to avoid the bad half.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Elitism**: exact champion copy
2-5. **Exploitation of champion**: small variants
6-14. **Exploration**: multi-state machines with nest-return logic

FSM designs:

- 2-state: GoToColor(GREEN) → on black → GoToColor(RED); on white → GoToColor(GREEN)
- 2-state: GoToColor(GREEN) → on black → GoAwayColor(BLUE); on white → back
- 3-state: Explore → on black GoToColor(GREEN); on white GoToColor(RED)
- GoAwayColor(BLUE) as primary

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Rounds 1 & 2:**

1. **Champion** (score -1.71): Single-state GoToColor(GREEN, clr=2). Simple but effective.
2. **2nd best** (score -1.10): 3-state machine: Explore → on seeing GREEN go to GoToColor(GREEN) → when on black go to GoToColor(RED). The state 1 (GoToColor GREEN) transitions back to itself when on black (wrong - destination must be different state). Wait, re-reading: state 1 goes to state 1 when on black... no, it transitions to state 1 (index 1 in "others" from state 1). Actually from state 1, others are [0, 2], so n1x0 with D=1 means state 2. Let me re-read: `--n1x0 1 --c1x0 0` means destination index 1 in others-list from state 1. Others from state 1 (of 3 states) = [0, 2], so D=1 → state 2 (GoToColor RED). Good - when on BLACK floor (at source), go toward RED (nest). When on WHITE (at nest), go back to state 0.
3. **Bad performers**: Two-state machines with GoToColor(GREEN)→black→GoToColor(RED) consistently score very poorly (1.78 to 17.63). The issue: when they transition to GoToColor(RED) immediately upon entering the black floor, they leave before picking up an item! The single-state champion works because it stays at green continuously.

**Key insight**: The 2-state GoToColor(GREEN)→GoToColor(RED) approach DOESN'T work because transitions on black floor fire immediately, before item pickup can occur. The champion works because it just keeps going to green and naturally returns to nest sometimes.

**Better approach**: 
- The 3-state explore→GoToColor(GREEN)→GoToColor(RED) that scored -1.10 works better because the explore state provides randomness.
- Try: GoToColor(GREEN) with transition on WHITE floor (at nest = delivered) → then go back. Or use random probability transitions.

**What to try:**
1. Keep champion
2. Exploit the 3-state -1.10 machine with small tweaks
3. Try GoAwayColor(BLUE) combined with GoToColor(GREEN)
4. Try GoToColor(GREEN) with low-probability random transition to GoToColor(RED)
5. Use floor-color sensing more cleverly: transition on WHITE (at nest) not BLACK (at source)

The key failure of 2-state machines: transitioning on BLACK floor takes robots away from source before collecting. Better: transition on WHITE floor (already delivered) back to GoToColor(GREEN).

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 6 --clr0 2

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The new champion (score -3.10) is a 3-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50) with 3 transitions: see GREEN→state1, on BLACK→state1, on WHITE→state1
- State 1: GoToColor(GREEN) with 3 transitions: on BLACK→state1(self? no...), on WHITE→state0, see BLUE→state1(self?)
- State 2: GoToColor(RED/nest)

Wait, let me re-read the champion carefully:
- State 0: Explore → transitions: (see GREEN color2, p=0.75→s1), (on BLACK p=0.45→s1), (on WHITE p=0.2→s1)
- State 1: GoToColor(GREEN) → transitions: (on BLACK p=0.92→s1 which is... others from s1 in 3-state are [0,2], so D=1→s2), (on WHITE p=0.55→s0), (see BLUE p=0.7→s1 which is... D=1→s2)
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) → transitions: (on WHITE p=0.9→s0), (on GRAY p=0.2→s1)

So the logic is:
- Explore until GREEN visible or on black/white → GoToColor(GREEN)
- At GoToColor(GREEN): when on black (at source, picked up item) → GoToColor(RED/nest)
- Also when seeing BLUE → go to RED (avoid blue)
- At GoToColor(RED): when on white (delivered) → back to Explore
- When on gray → back to GoToColor(GREEN)

This makes perfect sense! The key insight: transition from GoToColor(GREEN) on BLACK floor triggers going to nest. The earlier failed 2-state machines had the same logic but state 1 in 2-state means n1x0 with D=0 points back to state 0 which is GoToColor(GREEN) not GoToColor(RED).

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Small tweaks to champion: adjust probabilities (0.92→0.95, 0.55→0.5, etc.)
3. Try rwm variations (30, 40, 60, 70, 80)
4. Try 4-state extensions

**What to avoid:**
- Starting with GoToColor(GREEN) as state 0 (scores poorly)
- Transitions using random probability (5) on WHITE/BLACK detection
- Starting state GoToColor without explore phase

**Key pattern to exploit:**
- State 0: Explore
- State 1: GoToColor(GREEN) 
- State 2: GoToColor(RED)
- Transitions: s0→s1 on see-green or on-black; s1→s2 on on-black or see-blue; s2→s0 on on-white; s2→s1 on on-gray

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The pattern is very clear now. The top machines all share this structure:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=30-50) with 3-4 transitions: see GREEN→s1, on BLACK→s1, on WHITE→s1 (and optionally see BLUE→s1)
- State 1: GoToColor(GREEN) with 3 transitions: on BLACK→s2, on WHITE→s0, see BLUE→s2
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) with 2 transitions: on WHITE→s0, on GRAY→s1

**Key findings:**
- rwm=30 beats rwm=50 (champion). Try rwm=10, 15, 20, 25 to explore lower values.
- Adding 4th transition in state 0 (see BLUE→s1) slightly helps (rank 2: -3.70)
- The 4-state machine with GoAwayColor(BLUE) as state 2 scored poorly (-0.68) — the GoAwayColor detour hurts
- State 2 with extra transition (GoToColor RED → see GREEN → back to s1) scored -1.15, slightly worse
- Core probabilities: p(see-green)=0.75, p(on-black)=0.45, p(on-white)=0.2 for state 0; p(on-black)=0.92, p(on-white)=0.55, p(see-blue)=0.7 for state 1; p(on-white)=0.9, p(on-gray)=0.2 for state 2

**Exploitation:** Fine-tune the champion with rwm variations (10, 15, 20, 25, 30) and small probability adjustments.

**Exploration:** Try 4-state machines more carefully; try different transition probability combinations.

The champion has rwm=30. Let me try rwm=10, 15, 20, 25 and also mix probabilities.

Also try: state 1 with higher p(on-black) (0.95-0.99) to ensure robots go to nest as soon as they're at source.

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**

- rwm=1 and rwm=2 both achieve -6.95 (tied champion) — very low turning rate is best
- rwm=3 gives -6.38, rwm=5 gives -5.78 to -6.05 with 4 transitions
- The 4-transition state 0 (adding see-BLUE detection) is slightly worse than 3-transition for rwm=1,2 but competitive
- The champion uses rwm=1 with 3 transitions in state 0

**What to focus on:**
1. Keep champion (rwm=1, 3 transitions)
2. Try probability fine-tuning around champion values
3. The champion probabilities: p(see-green)=0.75, p(on-black)=0.45, p(on-white)=0.2 for state 0; p(on-black)=0.92, p(on-white)=0.55, p(see-blue)=0.7 for state 1; p(on-white)=0.9, p(on-gray)=0.2 for state 2

**Exploitation:** 
- Vary p values around champion: try p(on-black in s1)=0.85, 0.90, 0.95 for state 1's first transition
- Try p(on-white in s1)=0.5, 0.6 for state 1's second transition  
- Try p(on-white in s2)=0.85, 0.95 for state 2's first transition
- Try p(see-green)=0.7, 0.8, 0.85 in state 0
- Try p(on-black in s0)=0.4, 0.5 in state 0

**Exploration:**
- Try 4-state version with rwm=1 (combining best elements)
- Try adding extra transition in state 1 (see-green → stay? No... see-green is what we want to do)
- Try state 2 with 3 transitions (add on-black → return to s1 to re-grab item)

The score is converging around -7. Let me focus heavily on exploitation with slight probability variations.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (-6.95) from round 6 holds. This round's best was -6.88. The scores are converging around -6 to -7. Key observations:

1. **Best in round 7**: p(on-black in s1)=0.95 scored -6.88 (slight improvement direction from 0.92)

4. **Lower p(see-green in s0)=0.7, p(on-black in s0)=0.4**: -6.79 (competitive)

- Try p(on-white in s1) = 0.5, 0.45 (slightly lower)

**Key insight**: The tournament is noisy (same machine scores differently). Focus on the structural optimum we've found and fine-tune probabilities.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
